  I'm english and it's my first language, and I honestly can't tell the difference between TH and F. They sound exactly the same to me. I think it could be to do with the fact I was brought up by non-english parents.
    ↳ **@emmanuela.2932** (👍0 · 2016-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CMmy1rssjj4&lc=UggDdhHOo7ShLXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-Hahn88WpLCcJ8hA
      +PeppermintDonuts so, "death" and "deaf" sounds the same in your mouth? That's wrong, I mean, everything is allowed in matter of accents but... in a compact language like english, those losses of information are critical. My language is spanish. Proper spanish differentiates the letters "S" and "C"/"Z" with different sounds. "C" and "Z" are differentiated with the "TH" sound. But we, spanish speakers in the Americas, don't use that sound, we simply use the same sound for "S". That loss of information is not critical, our language is not compact, we have a lot of vowels to construct the words. English is more compact, you need proper consonant pronunciation.

  the first, "has", is a helping verb, it helps form the tense you want to use. the second, "had", is carrying the meaning of your sentence and could be replaced by: spent, forgotten etc.
  
  the same for "had had" - the first "had" is only a helping verb, the second "had" carries the meaning and could be replaced by some other verb.

**@ibribeh8547** (👍1 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=U304CZiFgO0&lc=UghqWRxqmJJvlXgCoAEC
  Can someone answer my question.
  right now I'm studying future form. It says here in my textbook that I must use present simple when I am to speak about fixed future events. e.g,: My train arrives at 5:00pm. But then why can I not say 'He is 18 years by old next month', rather than 'He will 18 by next month', or 'He is turning 18 next month'?
  I'm tired of native speakers telling me "that's just how it is" or "that's just how we say it". There must be some logical explanation.
    ↳ **@higashifullmoon04** (👍4 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=U304CZiFgO0&lc=UghqWRxqmJJvlXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-0cGl70etsuG7i_D
      the answer is simple, well it is for me lol, is the birthday a routine? a fixed schedule? In the example you provided we are talking about an schedule, one about the future; like saying "Monday, I teach English class, Tuesday I carry out minor tasks, Wednesday back to school" a fixed schedule written about other days, days that are on the future. However, turning 18 is not a routine, is a one time action that doesn't repeat.
      
      Remember, will is used for predictions.
      Going to and a verb is used to talk about plans and activities
      simple present, our routines, is used to talk about a routine for tomorrow
    ↳ **@cityhunterfan** (👍2 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=U304CZiFgO0&lc=UghqWRxqmJJvlXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-0cGl74_FUUK44qS
      Ibri Beh Here's my take on this. "He is 18 years by old next month", is future tense yes, but... it's a prediction. Yes, provided that the person lives, 'he will be 18...", but here's the core point, generally, English does not use the present continuous to predict something that is outside of our control. Instead, we use 'going to' or 'will': We can control when a train arrives.
      
      For instance, we say “The sun will rise in the morning.” This is a statement that is a prediction. If I were to say, “The sun rises in the morning,” I'm not making a prediction, but rather am stating a fact about the sun’s movement.
      Your stating, “He is 18 years old next month” (paraphrased), I can only see used in the context of correcting a fact:
      Question: He’s 18? (Meaning, is he 18 now)
      Answer: He’s 18 next month. (Correcting the fact that he is not 18 now)
      Here, there isn’t a prediction being made, but a correction on the age. And even this I think is more in colloquial usage.
      When using the present continuous for things/circumstances beyond human control, use ‘will’ or ‘going to’.
    ↳ **@nehagarg8683** (👍2 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=U304CZiFgO0&lc=UghqWRxqmJJvlXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-0cGl75GTzk-j4yz
      Ibri Beh Let us look at the present simple again, usually we use  present simple for fixed schedules because they are repetitive actions. For example:-let us say that the flight is supposed to depart at 6 P.M , so it is not just for one time, The same flight departs at same time every day unless something unexpected happens.
      
      However, you don't turn 18 every now & then. Do you? This is why you must say it in present continuous.
      
      I hope it satiates your curiosity:)
      
      Neha
      English language & communication skills trainer (India)

    ↳ **@blackcurrant87** (👍0 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=U304CZiFgO0&lc=UghqWRxqmJJvlXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-0cGl70rvJXcSoq8
      Ibri Beh You've made me think on this one!Firstly, 'by next month' sounds like an action is going to be completed in that time because 'by' suggests something is happening before next month. If you wanted to use 'by' in the sentence you'll have to use 'he will be 18 by next month'. This is because in this case we don't know the date the birthday is happening, we only know it is happening before next month. We have to use the future (with 'will') because we are making a prediction.Without using 'by', what I would say are correct sentences would be variants of:He will be 18 next monthHe is going to be 18 next month He is turning 18 next monthThose are the ones I would use (UK English). The future tense using 'going to + verb' is used for things that are very likely to happen. 'He is 18 next month' - Although this is widely used in spoken English, I struggled to find any grammar notes online about it. I guess you could argue (like higashi above/below) that changing your age is a regular occurrence (every year) and therefore it's acceptable to use the present tense. Hopefully that helps somewhat.

  My objective--as well as personal--reasons why fighting is a good thing are:
  
  
  1) To fight for what you believe in.
  2) To deliver justice if the reason(s) behind it is/are justified. It's human to be justifiably angry.
  3) Above all else, to prevent people from creating more victims!

**@rosycastillo3787** (👍0 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KMIkuRW864U&lc=UgjekkN4GGjjhXgCoAEC
  Hey! I have a question, what is the rule for doubling the last letter on a word when adding ly for example on the word GENERAL which becomes GENERALLY
    ↳ **@appseudo** (👍0 · 2016-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KMIkuRW864U&lc=UgjekkN4GGjjhXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-4MOS89Nh3qBw6jd
      +Rosy Castillo For anyone with the same doubt, the rule is: if an adjective ends in a "L", you just add "LY" and create an adverb. For exampe: General+ly=Generally; Especial+ly=especially; Actual+ly=Actually; Careful+ly=Carefully. As you can see, the letter is not doubled at all :)

  Merci Dylane !  I’ve subscribed to Babbel and Memrise, and agree with your comments for both.  I’ve never tried LingoPie, but it sounds interesting since my goal is to watch French TV and movies without subtitles.  My other goal is to learn real spoken French, the way French people actually speak to friends and family, rather than « school French » or written French.  So, with the exception of LingoPie, I think there is so much free content on YouTube that the other apps are not really worth the money.  The main disadvantage that I have found with YouTube free content is that there is usually not any guidance on how to structure the videos…that is, what order is best to watch them for a more logical learning experience.  Thank you for your videos and for these reviews !

**@anthonys2021-BD** (👍11 · 2023-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3Foxj0ng-Cg&lc=Ugxv3YDQj99lR3lc-Ip4AaABAg
  I have both Lingo Pie and Memrise. I like both a lot. I like Lingo Pie for all of the reasons you stated, Dylane. For Memrise, I like that I can scroll and select tenses, words, phrases etc, specific to certain subjects. And I can do this without needing to complete ALL courses on one level before exploring a different level. I also like it because I hear the same phrase from different native speakers, and it makes a difference for us learners.  I also like that it bounces from audio to written phrases to test the user. I am a little concerned that you found errors, but I like the videos in "Learn with Locals" best. In theory, those video clips are okay, even if they don't say the exact words written sometimes--like "chui" instead of "je suis."  I find that okay because I expect it.  But that's just me. I realize French-speaking natives shorten, clip, and truncate as we do in English.

  At 6:12 - The Beelinguapp audio is all done by computer. It is absolutely not perfect, BUT… it is really good for 95% of what it does.
  
  I would look at it like a tool. I would not expect my toaster to make juice. That’s not what it is for. My toaster is good at making toast, that’s it.
  
  It also isn’t perfect. Sometimes it over cooks something, but the majority of the time it is really good at what it does.
  
  That is Beelinguapp. 
  
  It is a really useful tool to practice reading and listening. It is especially good for learning whole phrases in context, BUT… it is not perfect.
  
  A live French teacher is best, but this is a helpful supplemental tool.

  私は日本人です。以前、lingoPie を使いましたが「フランス語→日本語」にすると文章の、意味が分かんなかったです。フランス語の原文の中に日本語が頻繁に混じる、例えば「Je fais venir mon フレール、キ、」、こんなのが多くて意味わからず。ところが「フランス語→→英語」にすると、「Je  fais venir mon frere」と、ちゃんとした翻訳でした。従って、フランス語→→‐英語→→日本語、これをしないと「日本語交じりの原文が出てきて、一旦、英語を訳してから、フランス語を訳するということをしてました。従って、lingoPie は、日本人にとっては不完全なアプリという印象でした。

**@paulharvey7278** (👍26 · 2023-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3Foxj0ng-Cg&lc=Ugw-GOYCgAEmjJppVGJ4AaABAg
  For me nothing can compare to the videos done (and also podcasts done) by such great French teachers online, starting with you Dylane, and many others that can easily be discovered. I follow their videos, and of course you can then choose to buy the programs they offer (like I have done with your books!). I'm on German now, my 3rd language, and I have learned so much from my own mistakes in previous language learning, like wasting a year in Spanish and not being able to speak at all at the end of that (I was using a program similar to Babbel, which is indeed great but you can't just use that, it has to be supplemented with your own readings, videos, language partners, etc). I agree that many of these apps are very good, especially for A1 - B1/2, I'm just working through the mysteries of basic German grammar (mon dieu) with Babbel and Busuu now (and like 5 minutes of DuoLingo every day just to warm up), but ultimately there is no substitute for extended listening and interaction with others. à bientôt Beefy !

**@danielh9844** (👍4 · 2023-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3Foxj0ng-Cg&lc=UgxCcHlPQ_BkGqCh91R4AaABAg
  One reason I really liked Busuu (at first) is one of your cons: At the A1 level there is a lot of repetition, and there is also a review after every several lessons (every 5, if I remember correctly). But after A1, the repetition and lesson reviews mainly go away. The assumption seems to be if you've seen something once, you never need to study it again. That's not how I learn. 
  
  After A1, I began encountering more and more mistakes in their modules. It seems their quality control procedure is to wait for their customers to point these out.
  
  Their language exchange can be helpful and annoying. If you log in for even a split second, Busuu immediately asks you to correct the submissions of someone who is studying your native language. So each time you sit down, for instance, to study French, the app is asking you to review someone's English.
  
  Now that I'm somewhere around B1 in French, it would be nice to find an app that is rich in content (sorry, Duolingo) and would allow me to easily skip to that level. Most apps aren't designed that way. At least, none that I've tried have come close to your ever-expanding content.  Another book coming.-- how do you do it?

**@LisaDiguardi** (👍5 · 2024-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3Foxj0ng-Cg&lc=UgziKQA-PbHkuS4wW8h4AaABAg
  I used a combination of Memrise and Coffee Break French, and if I had it to do over again I wouldn't change a thing. The videos where you haven't already reviewed all the vocabulary were a real advantage in training myself to listen for the main idea. The speed review in Memrise helped me get past constantly hesitating, because when I was trying to go quickly instead of second-guessing myself, I found I was usually right and it was a huge boost to my confidence. I also liked being able to continue after finishing the official courses by using the community-created courses, I am currently on courses that are French only (no English), including one for advanced medical terminology. Those can be used while doing the official courses, such as one that was a French-French course on memorizing world capitals.

  I personally use Rosetta Stone in conjunction with French grammar books as well as watching your videos to help break down different concepts. But I also use French grammar workbooks on the side as well as watching your videos. My goal is to read, speak, listen, and write fluently in French and I feel that the combination of Rosetta Stone plus studying my grammar books as well as watching your videos and watching tv in French has really advanced my French quite a bit!
